Global Phase Helps in Quantum Search: Yet Another Look at the Welded Tree Problem
Aleksandrs Belovs

Abstract
Up to now, relatively few exponential quantum speed-ups have been achieved. Out of them, the welded tree problem [ 10 ] is one of the unusual examples, as the exponential speed-up is attained by a quantum walk. In this paper, we give a very short proof of the optimal linear hitting time for this problem by a discrete-time quantum walk, which is based on a simple modification of the electric quantum walk framework [ 6 ] . The same technique can be applied to other 1-dimensional hierarchical graphs, yielding results similar to [ 4 ] .



1
Introduction

The usual behaviour of a random walk is that it mixes to the steady state.
This is caused by the exponential decay of the coefficients of the eigenvectors with small eigenvalues.
Quantum walks, on the other hand, are unitary operators, and they preserve the weight of its entire spectrum.
This leads to the following interesting phenomenon.
If the graph is carefully crafted, a quantum walk initiated at one specific “focal” vertex can efficiently traverse the whole graph – even if it is huge – and concentrate in some other “focal” vertex.

In early 2000s, this observation was used by Kempe
[
13
]
and Childs, Farhi, and Gutmann
[
11
]
to construct quantum walks exponentially outperforming their random counterparts.
Kempe
[
13
]
used a Boolean hypercube with the focal vertices being its two opposite vertices.
Childs
et al.

[
11
]
used two complete binary trees of depth
n
glued at the leaves with the focal vertices being the roots of the trees.

Although the above two papers demonstrate an exponential separation between quantum and random
walks
, they do not establish a similar separation between quantum and random
algorithms
, as the corresponding search problems can be efficiently solved even by a deterministic algorithm
[
10
]
.
This lead Childs, Cleve, Deotto, Farhi, Gutmann, and Spielman
[
10
]
to the formulation of the welded tree problem.
Unlike glued trees, the leaves of the two complete binary trees in the welded tree problem are joined by a random cycle (see Section
2.3
for more detail).
Given a root of one of the trees, the task is to find the root of the other tree.
The quantum walk still traverses this graph with ease, while no randomised algorithm can solve this problem in sub-exponential time.
This results in an exponential quantum speed-up.

Childs at el.
[
10
]
used a
continuous-time
quantum walk, and showed that its execution for time
O(n^{9/2})
is sufficient to find the root of the opposite tree, where
n
is the depth of the tree.
It was later improved to
O(n^{2}\log n)
by Atia and Chakraborty
[
3
]
.
Li, Li, and Luo
[
16
]
proved that analogous
discrete-time
quantum walk requires
O(n^{3/2})
iterations for the same task.
Balasubramanian, Li, and Harrow
[
4
]
generalised the continuous-time quantum walk of
[
10
]
to other types of hierarchical graphs.
While the algorithms in all these papers are based on standard quantum walks, rather elaborate spectral analysis is required to prove their correctness.

Discrete-time quantum walks are generally better suited for implementation in the traditional quantum circuit model as they avoid a complicated task of simulating a Hamiltonian.
Also, in many cases, they avoid spectral analysis.
In a seminal paper, Szegedy
[
17
]
showed that a discrete-time quantum walk attains a quadratic speed-up for detecting presence of marked vertices when initiated in the steady state of the random walk.
Electric quantum walk by Belovs
[
6
]
is a generalisation of this result for arbitrary initial distribution.
Applicable to any bipartite graph
G
, electric quantum walk can detect presence of marked vertices in
O(\sqrt{WR})
iterations, where
W
is the total weight of the graph, and
R
is the effective resistance between the initial and the marked vertices (see Section
3
for definitions).
It is relatively easy to estimate both quantities, which allows development of quantum algorithms by purely combinatorial means
[
5
,
15
,
7
]
.
A big drawback of this approach is that
O(WR)
captures hitting time of a
random
walk, which means that electric quantum walks do not provide more than a quadratic speed-up.

This quadratic barrier was recently broken by Jeffery and Zur
[
12
]
in a very interesting way.
They extended electric quantum walks with alternative neighbourhoods of vertices.
The algorithm can “guess” the correct neighbourhood of each vertex out of several options.
Additional constraints on the flow are to be satisfied to compensate for this guessing ability.
Using these techniques, it was possible to modify (in a rather intricate way) the welded tree graph achieving
O(n)
quantum hitting time.
This is clearly optimal, as such is the distance between the opposite roots.

In this paper, we give a much simpler proof of the same optimal
O(n)
upper bound on quantum hitting time on the welded tree graph.
Unlike Jeffery and Zur
[
12
]
, and similarly to Li, Li, and Luo
[
16
]
, we use the conventional version of discrete-time quantum walk on this graph.
Also, unlike Li, Li, and Luo, and similarly to Jeffery and Zur, we avoid any spectral analysis and use technique that is closely related to electric quantum walks.
Surprisingly, the only thing required to break the aforementioned quadratic barrier is to change the global phase of the walk!
The result is as follows, and is proven in Section
4
.

Theorem 1

(Informal)
.

O(n)
iterations of a standard discrete-time quantum walk on a welded tree graph of depth
n
are sufficient to find the vertex of the opposite tree.

The discrete-time quantum walk by Jeffery and Zur extensively uses the structure of the welded tree graph, and it is not apparent how to generalise it to other graphs.
Our algorithm can be applied to other graphs, which we demonstrate by analysing quantum hitting time on 1-dimensional hiearchical graphs.

Unlike random walks, in quantum walks, there is a substantial difference between ability to detect presence of marked vertices, and actually finding one.
Szegedy’s original paper
[
17
]
considered the problem of finding a marked vertex provided it is unique.
This case was later resolved by Krovi, Magniez, Ozols, and Roland
[
14
]
using so-called interpolating quantum walk.
Ambainis, Gilyén, Jeffery, and Kokainis
[
1
]
showed that quantum search is as fast as detection for arbitrary number of marked vertices, provided the initial state is the steady state like in Szegedy’s case.
Finally, Apers, Gilyén, and Jeffery
[
2
]
obtained the same result for arbitrary initial distribution.

The proofs of these results are rather sophisticated.
Jeffery and Zur
[
12
]
do not use any of these, but rely on a witty trick combining detection and the Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm.
Although this trick is not complicated and is very general, it does add an additional layer of sophistication to the algorithm.
We avoid this step, and solve the search problem directly.
In fact, we demonstrate an algorithm for finding unique marked vertex using electric quantum walk.
This algorithm is not as general the one by Apers, Gilyén, and Jeffery
[
2
]
, but its analysis is much simpler.

We make use of a novel approach to quantum walks using transducers
[
8
]
.
Although the same results can be obtained using more traditional phase estimation, we find the approach using transducers substantially cleaner.

The paper is organised as follows.
In Section
2.1
, we define transducers and their implementation by a quantum algorithm.
Section
2.2
defines electric quantum walk, and sketches its implementation using diffuse-and-shift approach.
We modify the quantum walk slightly to capture the search problem.
In Section
2.3
, we define the welded tree problem.

In Section
3
, we show how to solve the search problem using electric quantum walks provided the marked vertex is unique.
In Section
4
, we prove Theorem
1
.
We note that it is not based on the walk in Section
3
, the latter being given for comparison and to provide more intuition.
Finally, in Section
5
, we apply the same technique for the case of general 1-dimensional hierarchical graphs.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Transducers

The use of transducers
[
8
]
is a novel approach to the design of quantum algorithms based on the ideas from
[
9
]
.
The following easy linear-algebraic result is the key to their definition.

Theorem 2
.

Let
S
be a unitary that acts in a direct sum of two vectors spaces
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.
For every
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, there exist
\tau=\tau(S,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}
and
v=v(S,\xi)\in\mathcal{L}
such that

S\colon\xi\oplus v\mapsto\tau\oplus v.

(1)

Moreover, the vector
\tau
is uniquely specified by
S
and
\xi
, and the mapping
\xi\mapsto\tau(S,\xi)
is unitary.

In these settings, we call
S
a transducer and say that
S

transduces

\xi
into
\tau
, denoted
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
.
The motivation is that while
S
does
not
literally map
\xi
into
\tau
, having
S
is a legitimate way of implementing this transformation, as we show shortly.
The vector
v
is called the
catalyst
of this transduction, and
W(S,\xi)=\|v(S,\xi)\|^{2}
is
transduction complexity
.
The spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}
are called the
public
and the
private
spaces of the transducer
S
, respectively.
The following theorem states that we can efficiently perform transduction if we allow for a small error.

Theorem 3
.

Let vector spaces
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{L}
, and a positive integer
K
be fixed.
There exists a quantum algorithm that transforms
\xi
into a vector
\tau^{\prime}
such that

\|\tau^{\prime}-\tau(S,\xi)\|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{W(S,\xi)}{K}}

for every transducer
S\colon\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
and every initial state
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
.
The algorithm uses
K
controlled applications of
S
and
O(K)
other elementary operations.

Therefore, if we allow a constant error, it suffices to execute the transducer
O(W)
times, where
W
is an upper bound on the transduction complexity
W(S,\xi)
.

2.2
Electric quantum walks

In this section, we define electric quantum walk on a graph, and sketch its implementation.
The analysis will be performed in Section
3
.
We will be interested in the search problem, hence, our exposition will be slightly different from the one in
[
6
]
.
For simplicity, we assume unique initial vertex.
The general case of an arbitrary initial probability distribution can be obtained in a way similar to
[
6
]
.
The assumption of the unique
marked
vertex is essential to the construction, though.

The walk takes place on a bipartite graph
G
with parts
A
and
B
, and the edge set
E
.
Each edge
e\in E
is assigned a non-negative real weight
w_{e}
.
The walk starts in some vertex
s\in A
.
Exactly one vertex
t\in B
is marked.
The goal is to find the marked vertex
t
.

For the quantum walk, we build the extended graph
G^{\prime}
as follows, see Figure
2.2
.
We add two new vertices
s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
to the graph, as well as dangling edges
ss^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}t
.
1
1

1

The edge
ss^{\prime}
is adopted from
[
6
]
.
The edge
t^{\prime}t
is similar to the self-loop on a marked vertex in the interpolating quantum walk of Krovi
et al.

[
14
]
and the subsequent papers.

Note that
s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
are neither in
A
, nor in
B
.
Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of the two newly added dangling edges.
Each edge in
E^{\prime}
has weight 1.

A
s
B
t
w_{e}
t^{\prime}
s^{\prime}
1
1

The space of the quantum walk is
\mathbb{C}^{E\cup E^{\prime}}
, where we treat
\mathbb{C}^{E^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{C}^{E}
as the public and the private space, respectively, in the sense of Section
2.1
.
For brevity, we will denote
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|ss^{\prime}}}\right>
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t^{\prime}t}}\right>
.
We assume each edge
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>
is given some orientation.
Precise choice of orientation is not important, as its change merely results in change of the sign:
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|uv}}\right>=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|vu}}\right>
.
For concreteness, however, we will assume all the edges are oriented towards the vertices in
B
and away from the vertices in
A
.

For each
u\in A\cup B
, define the following
star state
:

\psi_{u}=\sum_{e\sim u}\sqrt{w_{e}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>,

(2)

where the sum is over all the edges incident to
u
(including the new edges from
E^{\prime}
).
We note that this simple form of the state
\psi_{u}
is due to our choice of the orientation: either all the edges are oriented towards
u
, or from
u
.
Should the edges vary in direction, this would have been represented by different signs in (
2
).

Definition 4
.

One step (or iteration) of the quantum walk
on the above extended graph
G^{\prime}
is defined as
U=U(G^{\prime})=R_{B}(G^{\prime})R_{A}(G^{\prime})
, where
R_{A}=R_{A}(G^{\prime})
(resp.,
R_{B}=R_{B}(G^{\prime})
) denotes the reflection of all
\psi_{u}
where
u
ranges over
A
(resp.,
B
).
In other words,
R_{A}
is defined as negation on the span of
\{\psi_{u}\}_{u\in A}
and identity on its orthogonal complement, and
R_{B}
is defined similarly for
\{\psi_{u}\}_{u\in B}
.

Sometimes we talk about quantum walk on
G
when we actually mean quantum walk on
G^{\prime}
.

Let us make some notes on the implementation of
U
.
In general, we need not assume the algorithm knows the whole graph.
The following three primitives are essential, however:

•

The algorithm knows the initial vertex
s
.

•

For any
u\in A\cup B
, the algorithm can test whether
u
is marked.

•

For any
u\in A\cup B
, the algorithm can learn the neighbourhood of
u
.
More specifically, it is possible to prepare the state
\psi_{u}
from (
2
) in order to implement
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
.

It may seem one has to know whether a vertex
u
is in
A
or
B
in order to understand which
\psi_{u}
to reflect.
But this can be circumvented using the following standard construction.
Let
K_{2}
be the complete graph on two vertices
0
and
1
.
Consider the tensor product
G\times K_{2}
of the graphs.
Its vertex set is
V\times\{0,1\}
with
V=A\cup B
being the vertex set of
G
.
There is an edge between
(u,a)
and
(v,b)
in
G\times K_{2}
iff
uv
is an edge of
G
, and
a\neq b
.
The weight of this edge is equal to the weight of
uv
in
G
.

The graph
G\times K_{2}
is bipartite with parts
V\times\{0\}
and
V\times\{1\}
even if
G
is not bipartite.
If
G
is bipartite,
G\times K_{2}
breaks down into two disconnected components, each isomorphic to
G
: the first one with the parts
A\times\{0\}
and
B\times\{1\}
, and the second one with the parts
A\times\{1\}
and
B\times\{0\}
.
A (quantum) walk on the graph
G_{2}
with the initial vertex
(s,0)
only takes place in the first component, while the second one is inaccessible and can be ignored.
Therefore, in this case, quantum walks on
G
and
G\times K_{2}
are identical.

Exact details on how the edges are represented in memory and how the states
\psi_{u}
are prepared depend on particular application.
The following diffuse-and-shift approach is quite common, though.
The space of the walk is embedded into the space spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|u}}\right>\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>
with
u,v
in the vertex set
V
of
G
.
The state
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|u}}\right>\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>
represents the edge between
(u,0)
and
(v,1)
in
G\times K_{2}
.
The quantum walk alternates between the diffuse operation
D
, and the shift operation
S
.
The diffuse operation performs the reflection of the states

\psi_{(u,0)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|u}}\right>\otimes\sum_{v}\sqrt{w_{uv}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>

over all
u\in V
, where the sum is over all the vertices
v
incident to
u
.
The shift operation swaps the two registers.
It is easy to see that
D=R_{V\times\{0\}}
and
SDS=R_{V\times\{1\}}
, hence,
U=SDSD
equals one step of the quantum walk on
G\times K_{2}
.

The above discussion neglected the new dangling edges
ss^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}t
.
They can be represented as
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\perp}}\right>
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\perp}}\right>\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
, where
\perp
is some specific element outside of
V
.
The diffuse operation should be modified accordingly.
This can be done as the algorithm knows the vertex
s
, and can test whether a particular vertex is marked.

2.3
Welded Tree Problem

A welded tree graph
G_{n}
of depth
n
consists of two complete binary trees of depth
n
with a cycle between the leaves of the trees, see Figure
2.3
.
The cycle uses each of the leaves exactly once, and only uses edges between leaves from two different trees.
We call one tree left, and the other one right.
For each tree, we divide the edges into layers, where layer
1
is incident to the root, layer
n
is incident to the leaves, and, in general, the edges in layer
i
connect vertices at distance
i-1
to vertices at distance
i
from the root.
The edges connecting the leaves of both trees comprise the middle layer.
Each edge of the graph has weight 1.

The root of the left tree is the initial vertex
s
, and the root of the right tree is the marked vertex
t
.
This is a bipartite graph with parts we denote
A
and
B
.
The initial vertex
s
is in
A
, and the marked vertex
t
is in
B
, as required in Section
2.2
.
We modify the graph as described in the same section by adding two dangling edges
ss^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}t
, both of weight 1.

s^{\prime}
s
t
t^{\prime}
L_{1}
L_{2}
L_{3}
M
R_{3}
R_{2}
R_{1}

In the welded tree problem one has to find the node
t
given the node
s
.
The graph is accessible via an oracle that, given a vertex
u
in the graph, outputs a list of its three neighbours (or two neighbours in the case of
s
and
t
).
With this oracle, it is easy to implement quantum walk on the extended graph
G_{n}^{\prime}
using the diffuse-and-shift technique from the end of Section
2.2
.
For instance, it is possible to use the same algorithm as in
[
16
, Appendix A]
with minor modification to include the new dangling edges
ss^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}t
.
Even more, their addition makes the graph more uniform, as now all the original vertices of the graph have degree 3.

3
Simple Search Using Electric Quantum Walks

In this section, we give a simple extension of the electric quantum walk from
[
6
]
that finds a marked vertex provided it is unique.
For simplicity, we assume that the initial vertex of the quantum walk is also unique.
These conditions are satisfied in the welded tree problem.

Let

W=\sum_{e\in E}w_{e}

be the total weight of the graph, and let
R_{s,t}
be the minimum of

\sum_{e\in E}\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{w_{e}},

(3)

over all unit flows
p=(p_{e})
from
s
to
t
in the graph
G
.
The minimum is attained by the electrical flow, and
R_{s,t}
is the corresponding effective resistance.

Theorem 5
.

One step
U=R_{B}R_{A}
of the quantum walk transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>
into
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
with transduction complexity at most
\frac{1}{2}(W+R_{s,t})
.

Assuming we know an upper bound
W
on the total weight, and
R
on the effective resistance, we can get an algorithm that finds
t
in
O(\sqrt{WR})
iterations of the quantum walk using the following standard re-weighting trick.
Let
\alpha=\sqrt{R/W}
.
We convert
G
into the graph
\alpha G
by multiplying the weight of each edge
e\in E
by
\alpha
.
The new graph
\alpha G
has total weight upper bounded by
\alpha W
and the effective resistance by
R/\alpha
, which are both equal to
\sqrt{RW}
.
Now we can apply Theorems
5
and
3
.

Note that application of Theorem
3
introduces error, while transduction in Theorem
5
is exact.
The latter can be useful if the transducer
U
is composed with other transducers, see
[
8
]
.
The same applies to other transducers in this paper.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We will show that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle U}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\stackrel{{\scriptstyle U}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
, and obtain the final transduction by taking the linear combination of the two.
Both of these transductions closely follow the corresponding constructions for detection from
[
8
]
.

For the first transduction, the catalyst is the following sum of the edges:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>=\sum_{e\in E}\sqrt{w_{e}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>.

Note that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>=\sum_{u\in A}\psi_{u}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>=\sum_{u\in B}\psi_{u},

hence, by the definition of
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
, we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>.

(4)

For the second transduction, the catalyst is given by the flow:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>=\sum_{e\in E}\frac{p_{e}}{\sqrt{w_{e}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>.

Here
p=(p_{e})
is the unit flow from
s
to
t
in
G
attaining the minimum in (
3
).
This flow can be extended to the flow from
s^{\prime}
to
t^{\prime}
in the extended graph
G^{\prime}
by letting
p_{ss^{\prime}}=p_{t^{\prime}t}=-1
(as the flow goes in the direction opposite to the orientation of the edges).
The flow is preserved in each
u\in A\cup B
, therefore

-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\perp\spn_{u\in A\cup B}\psi_{u},

and

-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>.

(5)

By subtracting (
5
) from (
4
), we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\frac{v_{0}-v_{1}}{2}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle U}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>+\frac{v_{0}-v_{1}}{2}.

Thus,
U
transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>
into
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
with transduction complexity

\frac{\|v_{0}-v_{1}\|^{2}}{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\|v_{0}\|^{2}+\|v_{1}\|^{2}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(W+R_{s,t}\bigr).\qed

4
Welded Trees

First, we will show that the standard electric quantum walk of Theorem
5
requires exponential (in
n
) number of iterations to find
t
.
After that, we introduce a simple modification that reduces the required number of iterations all the way down to
O(n)
.

\hskip-12.80365pt\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>&-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\\

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

The states
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{0}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
from (
4
) and
-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{1}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
from (
5
) for this particular case are depicted in Figure
4
.
It is not hard to see that

\|v_{0}\|^{2}=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}2^{i}\cdot 1+2^{n+1}\cdot 1=\Theta(2^{n})\quad\text{and}\quad\|v_{1}\|^{2}=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}2^{i}\frac{1}{2^{2i}}+2^{n+1}\frac{1}{2^{2(n+1)}}=\Theta(1).

(6)

By Theorem
5
, this results in an exponential number of iterations.

The problem here is that the catalysts
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
are not balanced. The coefficients in the first sum in (
6
) decrease too slowly (rather, do not decrease at all), while the ones in the second sum decrease even too fast.
We solve this problem by considering a mixture of these two catalysts.
For simplicity, we assume
n
is odd.
The case of even
n
is similar, but slightly more complicated,
and is captured by a more general Theorem
8
.

\hskip-12.80365pt\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\\
\hbox to218.48pt{\vbox to119.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.07953pt\lower-56.7962pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}{{{{}}}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FF8080} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FF8080} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.87 0 C 5.87 3.24 3.24 5.87 0 5.87 C -3.24 5.87 -5.87 3.24 -5.87 0 C -5.87 -3.24 -3.24 -5.87 0 -5.87 C 3.24 -5.87 5.87 -3.24 5.87 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Theorem 6
.

Let
U=R_{B}R_{A}
be one step of the quantum walk on the welded tree graph of odd depth
n
.
Then
-U
transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>
into
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
with transduction complexity at most
3(n+1)
.

By Theorem
3
, it takes
O(n)
iterations of the quantum walk to find
t
.
Surprisingly, the only difference between operators used in Theorems
5
and
6
is the global phase, but it results in performance estimates that are exponentially far apart!

Proof of Theorem
6
.

Consider the following vector
v_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{E}
, see also Figure
4
:

v_{2}[\![e]\!]=\begin{cases}(-1/2)^{i},&\text{if $e$ belongs to level $2i$ or $2i+1$ of the left tree;}\\
(-1/2)^{i},&\text{if $e$ belongs to level $2i$ or $2i-1$ of the right tree.}\end{cases}

(7)

The middle layer between the trees can be considered as the
(n+1)
-st level of both trees, and both cases of (
7
) apply there (we are using that
n
is odd here).
This vector behaves as the sum of the edges for vertices in
A
, and as a flow for vertices in
B
.
In other words,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>\in\spn_{u\in A}\psi_{u}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\perp\spn_{u\in B}\psi_{u}.

This gives us

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>.

(8)

Similarly, we define
v_{3}\in\mathbb{C}^{E}
, which is a mirror image of
v_{2}
:

v_{3}[\![e]\!]=\begin{cases}(-1/2)^{i},&\text{if $e$ belongs to level $2i$ or $2i-1$ of the left tree;}\\
(-1/2)^{i},&\text{if $e$ belongs to level $2i$ or $2i+1$ of the right tree.}\end{cases}

Again, the middle layer is covered by both cases.
However, this time we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>\perp\spn_{u\in A}\psi_{u},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\in\spn_{u\in B}\psi_{u},

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>.

(9)

Combining (
8
) and (
9
), we get for
U=R_{B}R_{A}
:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\frac{v_{2}+v_{3}}{2}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-U}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>+\frac{v_{2}+v_{3}}{2},

(10)

which gives the required transduction.
We can estimate the norms of
v_{2}
and
v_{3}
as
two times the heaviest tree including the middle layer:

\|v_{2}\|^{2}=\|v_{3}\|^{2}\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{(n+1)/2}\Bigl(2^{2i-1}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{2(i-1)}}+2^{2i}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{2i}}\Bigr)=3(n+1).

(11)

From this, the bound on transduction complexity follows, as

\frac{\|v_{2}+v_{3}\|^{2}}{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\|v_{2}\|^{2}+\|v_{3}\|^{2}\bigr)\leq 3(n+1).\qed

5
Hierarchical 1-Dimensional Graphs

In this section, we apply the technique from Theorem
6
for 1-dimensional hierarchical graphs.
They are defined in
[
4
]
, and form a generalisation of all the examples mentioned in the introduction: the welded tree graph, the glued tree graph, and the hypercube.

Definition 7
.

The vertex set of a
1-dimensional hierarchical graph

G
is a disjoint union
S_{0}\sqcup S_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup S_{n}
with
|S_{0}|=|S_{n}|=1
.
The edge set is a disjoint union
L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup L_{n}
.
The edges in
L_{i}
form a bipartite graph with parts
S_{i-1}
and
S_{i}
, where each vertex in
S_{i-1}
has degree
d_{i-1}^{+}
, and each vertex in
S_{i}
has degree
d_{i}^{-}
.

In the corresponding
search problem
one has to find the sole vertex
t
in
S_{n}
given the sole vertex
s
in
S_{0}
.
It is assumed that there is an oracle that can tell whether a vertex
v
equals
t
.

The graph can only exist if
|S_{i-1}|d_{i-1}^{+}=|S_{i}|d_{i}^{-}=|L_{i}|
.
We will assume that
n
is odd.
2
2

2

The case of even
n
can be reduced to the odd case by adding one more layer of edges
E_{n+1}
and vertices
S_{n+1}
with
|E_{n+1}|=|S_{n+1}|=1
.

The graph
G
is bipartite with parts
A=S_{0}\cup S_{2}\cup\cdots\cup S_{n-1}
and
B=S_{1}\cup S_{3}\cup\cdots\cup S_{n}
.
We will use discrete-time quantum walk from Section
2.2
.
In particular, we extend the graph to
G^{\prime}
by adding two dangling edges
ss^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}t
.
We assume the weights of all the original edges in
G
are 1.
The weights of the edges
ss^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}t
are
W_{0}
and
W_{n+1}
, respectively.
We do not assume they are equal to 1 here, contrary to Section
2.2
.
We also denote
W_{i}=|L_{i}|
for all
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

We assume we can implement the transformation
U=R_{B}R_{A}
efficiently, and we will be interested in the number of applications of this unitary required to solve the search problem.
Let

C_{k}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{W_{i-1}}{W_{i}}}}\right)^{(-1)^{i}}=\frac{W_{1}}{W_{0}}\cdot\frac{W_{1}}{W_{2}}\cdot\frac{W_{3}}{W_{2}}\cdot\frac{W_{3}}{W_{4}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{W_{k-1}}{W_{k}}}}\right)^{(-1)^{k}}.

Assuming the graph
G
is fixed, for each choice of the weight
W_{0}
of
ss^{\prime}
, there is unique choice of the weight
W_{n+1}
of
t^{\prime}t
such that
C_{n+1}=1
.
The main result of this section is the following theorem, which is comparable to a similar result in
[
4
]
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be an extended 1-dimensional hierarchical graph as above such that
C_{n+1}=1
.
Denote by
U=R_{B}R_{A}
one step of the quantum walk on this graph.
Then
-U
transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>
into
(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>
with transduction complexity not exceeding

\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\Bigl(C_{k}+\frac{1}{C_{k}}\Bigr)}\biggr).

s^{\prime}
s_{0}
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{4}
s_{5}
s_{6}
s_{7}
t^{\prime}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{0}}}\right>
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{1}}}\right>
2
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2}}}\right>
4
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{3}}}\right>
8
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{4}}}\right>
16
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{5}}}\right>
8
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{6}}}\right>
4
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{7}}}\right>
2
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{8}}}\right>
1
(a)

s^{\prime}
s_{0}
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{4}
s_{5}
s_{6}
s_{7}
t^{\prime}
1
1
\sqrt{2}
2
-1
4
-\sqrt{2}
8
1
16
\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}
8
-1
4
-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}
2

1
(b)

s^{\prime}
s_{0}
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{4}

1
1
-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}
2

1
(c)

Before we prove the theorem, let us explain the standard technique of reducing a quantum walk on
G_{n}
to a quantum walk on the line, utilised in pretty much all previous papers
[
13
,
11
,
10
,
16
,
4
]
.
Let

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i}}}\right>=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|L_{i}|}}\sum_{e\in L_{i}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>.

We define the
flattened graph

\bar{G}
as follows.
It has vertices
s_{0},s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
.
The edge between
s_{i-1}
and
s_{i}
is given by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i}}}\right>
, and it has weight
W_{i}=|L_{i}|
.
This graph is bipartite with parts

\bar{A}=\{s_{0},s_{2},\dots s_{n-1}\}

and

\bar{B}=\{s_{1},s_{3},\dots s_{n}\}
.
Again, we extend the graph to
\bar{G}^{\prime}
by adding edges
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{0}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|ss^{\prime}}}\right>
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{n+1}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t^{\prime}t}}\right>
incident to
s_{0}
and
s_{n}
, respectively.
They have the same weight as in
G^{\prime}
:
W_{0}
and
W_{n+1}
, respectively.
See Figure
5
(a) for an example.

Proposition 9
.

Both operations
R_{A}(G^{\prime})
and
R_{B}(G^{\prime})
of the quantum walk step on the graph
G^{\prime}
, when restricted to the space spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{0}}}\right>,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{1}}}\right>,\cdots,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{n+1}}}\right>
, are identical to the operations
R_{\bar{A}}(\bar{G}^{\prime})
and
R_{\bar{B}}(\bar{G}^{\prime})
of the quantum walk on the graph
\bar{G}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For
i\in\{0,\dots,n\}
, we have

\sum_{u\in S_{i}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{u}}}\right>=\sqrt{W_{i}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i}}}\right>+\sqrt{W_{i+1}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i+1}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{s_{i}}}}\right>,

(12)

where the star states of the left are from
G^{\prime}
and on the right from
\bar{G}^{\prime}

Indeed, both sides are equal to

\sqrt{W_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|ss^{\prime}}}\right>+\sum_{e\in L_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>,\qquad\sum_{e\in L_{i-1}\cup L_{i}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\sum_{e\in L_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>+\sqrt{W_{n+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t^{\prime}t}}\right>

for
i=0
,
0<i<n
, and
i=n
, respectively.

Also,

\sqrt{W_{i+1}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i}}}\right>-\sqrt{W_{i}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i+1}}}\right>

is orthogonal to (
12
).
By symmetry, it has the same inner product with all
\psi_{u}
for
u\in S_{i}
, hence, it is orthogonal to all of them.
Therefore, in the space spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i}}}\right>
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{i+1}}}\right>
, the reflection of all
\psi_{u}
with
u\in S_{i}
is identical to the reflection of
\psi_{s_{i}}
.
The proposition follows from the definitions of
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8
.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
6
.
First, we can use Proposition
9
, and analyse the quantum walk on the graph
\bar{G}^{\prime}
instead.
Define

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor k/2}}\right\rfloor}\sqrt{C_{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{k}}}\right>,

where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor x}}\right\rfloor
is the floor operation.
We have (where we assume
C_{0}=1
):

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>=\sum_{j=0}^{(n-1)/2}(-1)^{j}\sqrt{C_{2j}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j}}}\right>+\sqrt{\frac{W_{2j+1}}{W_{2j}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j+1}}}\right>}}\right)\in\spn_{v\in\bar{A}}\psi_{v}.

On the other hand,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>+(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>=\sum_{j=1}^{(n+1)/2}(-1)^{j-1}\sqrt{C_{2j-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j-1}}}\right>-\sqrt{\frac{W_{2j-1}}{W_{2j}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j}}}\right>}}\right)\perp\spn_{v\in\bar{B}}\psi_{v}.

As in (
8
), this gives us

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{\bar{A}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{\bar{B}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{2}}}\right>-(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>.

(13)

Define also

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil k/2}}\right\rceil}\sqrt{\frac{1}{C_{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{k}}}\right>,

where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil x}}\right\rceil
is the ceiling operation.
Again, we have:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>=\sum_{j=0}^{(n-1)/2}(-1)^{j}\sqrt{\frac{1}{C_{2j}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j}}}\right>-\sqrt{\frac{W_{2j}}{W_{2j+1}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j+1}}}\right>}}\right)\perp\spn_{v\in\bar{A}}\psi_{v},

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>+(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>=\sum_{j=1}^{(n+1)/2}(-1)^{j}\sqrt{\frac{1}{C_{2j-1}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j-1}}}\right>+\sqrt{\frac{W_{2j}}{W_{2j-1}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|L_{2j}}}\right>}}\right)\in\spn_{v\in\bar{B}}\psi_{v}.

As in (
9
), this gives us

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>+(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>+(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{3}}}\right>-(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>.

(14)

Combining (
13
) and (
14
), and using Proposition
9
, we get for both
U=R_{B}(\bar{G}^{\prime})R_{A}(\bar{G}^{\prime})
and
U=R_{B}(G^{\prime})R_{A}(G^{\prime})
:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|s}}\right>+\frac{v_{2}+v_{3}}{2}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-U}}{{\longmapsto}}\;(-1)^{(n+1)/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>+\frac{v_{2}+v_{3}}{2},

(15)

which gives the required transduction with transduction complexity

\frac{\|v_{2}+v_{3}\|^{2}}{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\|v_{2}\|^{2}+\|v_{3}\|^{2}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\Bigl(C_{k}+\frac{1}{C_{k}}\Bigr)}\biggr).\qed

An example of this construction for the welded tree graph with depth
3
is given in Figure
5
.
This analysis is identical to the one done in Section

.

8
also gives an
O(n)
upper bound on the hitting time for welded tree graphs with even depth
n
.
For instance, Figure
5
features the case of even depth
2
.
